Therefore, the cool rays of the moon are compared to the teacher's compassion, and the white light of the moon is compared to the teacher's purity. The stars are compared to the students. Although stars appear small, they, like the moon, are self-luminous objects. Therefore, the comparison of students to stars implies that students, like teachers, are living beings with individual intellectual light. Furthermore, some stars emit more light, while others emit less. Similarly, the greatness and smallness of students are also conveyed through this simile. Moreover, when the moon appears, we can see the stars by the moon's light. Similarly, the simile beautifully suggests that students will gain intellectual enlightenment through the intellectual illumination of the teacher. Furthermore, the moon is one of the planets. The moon continuously emits light. Stars, on the other hand, emit light intermittently. Therefore, even in this sense, the beauty of comparing the teacher to the moon and the students to stars gives us joy. Moreover, the light of the moon and the light of the stars are together in the sky. This signifies the blending of the teacher's intellectual light and the students' intellectual light. Thus, Sekkizhar Peruman sings about that place where the Vedas are recited. There, young students wear deer skin and the triple sacred thread on their chests. Sekkizhar Peruman refers to the deer skin they wear as "Kothil maan thol," meaning faultless deer skin. Deer skin is taken from a dead animal, a deer. Therefore, Sekkizhar Peruman refers to it with the adjective "faultless skin." Furthermore, deer skin is traditionally considered pure. Thus, these young boys wear deer skin and the triple sacred thread on their chests. They have a tuft of hair. These are all the symbols of Brahmachari boys in the Brahmin community. Thus, Sekkizhar Peruman sings about the presence of Vedic schools in Thiruchengalur. He continues to sing about the Brahmins living in that holy place. There are sacrificial halls there. Sacrifices are performed in those sacrificial halls. In those sacrifices, oblations are offered to Brahma, Vishnu, Indra, and other deities. Vishnu, Brahma, and Indra come there to partake of the offered oblations. When they come, they arrive on their respective vehicles. The Garuda on which Vishnu rides, the swan on which Brahma rides, and the Airavata elephant on which Indra rides, are parked and tied to the Yupa Stambhas (sacrificial posts) located next to the sacrificial halls in that village. Those who come to the sacrificial halls for the sacrifices in their vehicles must park them next to the sacrificial halls. Therefore, the Yupa Stambhas serve this purpose. A Yupa is a pillar to which the sacrificial animal is tied. In Tamil, it is called "Omaththari." Sekkizhar Peruman sings, "Eendha avi unavin bhaagam," meaning the portion of the offered oblation. He sings that Vishnu, Indra, and Brahma have come there to receive the remaining portions of the oblation. He does not mention Lord Shiva here. This is because Lord Shiva is the chief of all sacrifices. Sacrifices performed without Lord Shiva, like the sacrifice performed by Daksha, will be destroyed and incomplete. Therefore, it is customary to first offer oblations to Lord Shiva and invite him before offering food to other deities. Furthermore, Vishnu, Brahma, and Indra will be present to protect the sacrifices dedicated to Shiva. Therefore, the oblation offered to Lord Shiva is understood without explicit mention. This is the underlying meaning. Moreover, since Sekkizhar Peruman mentions "Eendha avi unavin bhaagam" (the portion of the offered oblation), it is implied that what is given to them is the remainder after offering to Shiva. This is also an underlying meaning. Thus, at the end of the sacrifice, oblations are offered to them. Therefore, they must wait until then. Their vehicles must also wait until then. Therefore, they need a place for those vehicles to park. Sekkizhar Peruman sings in this verse that the Yupa Stambha serves this purpose. Now, Sekkizhar Peruman sings about the streets of Thiruchengalur. The streets of Thiruchengalur are very long. They are wide streets. They are streets without ups and downs. They are beautiful streets without bends, curves, or narrow alleys. There is good shade on both sides of the streets. Herds of cows walk along these streets throughout the day. As these cows walk along the streets, their udders are so full that milk flows out without being milked. The unhindered flow of milk is a sign of the cows' healthy growth. Furthermore, the milk flows unhindered because the cows are thinking of their calves as they walk along the streets. These cows are raised in the homes of Vedic scholars for the Panchagavyam (five products of the cow) needed for the homas (fire rituals) performed by the Vedic scholars. All of them are walking along those streets. It is implied here that if cows are raised healthily, they will yield milk without holding back. Furthermore, the story of Chandesura Nayanar is also mentioned here, as it resulted from the cows yielding milk without holding back. Moreover, in the streets of Thiruchengalur, students learning the Vedas gather samit (sacred twigs) for the homa. Bringing samit for the daily fire ritual is one of the duties assigned to students studying the Vedas. This was a custom among Brahmins of that era. Vedic schools are located outside the village. Students go there to learn the Vedas. When they return home in the evening after learning the Vedas, they bring back the necessary samit. These samit are for the daily fire ritual maintained in Brahmin homes. Thus, students who learn the Vedas in the Vedic schools outside the village and return home in the afternoon bring back the gathered samit. As they walk along the streets, they repeatedly recite and practice the portions of the Vedas they have learned. This is called "kaniththal" (calculating or reckoning). "Kaniththal" is the method of repeatedly reciting and practicing the learned portions of the Vedas and reckoning them. As we have already seen, because the measurements of pitches, words, Jata, Krama, and other methods, and the measurements of time are calculated and practiced, this is called "kanippathu" (calculating), and the young boys who do this are called "kanippor" (calculators). Thus, in the afternoon, the Vedic boys in the streets of Thiruchengalur memorize and practice the Vedas as they bring the gathered samit. The women in Brahmin homes go to the flower ponds outside the village, bathe, and bring water back to their homes. Sacrificial halls are set up in suitable places outside the village. Generally, there are specific places where sacrificial halls should be built. They should be outside the village, and next to them should be a river, fields, and ponds. Sacrificial halls should be built on their banks. Furthermore, there should be large areas around the sacrificial halls for chariots to stand. In Thiruchengalur, the Manniyaaru river flows outside the village, filled with water, with waves rolling. There are fields on the banks of the Manniyaaru river. Next to the field boundaries are banks with large open spaces. Next to these banks is a very large area. Beyond that is the city. Thus, sacrificial halls are built in the wide areas between the city and the field banks. There are open spaces around the sacrificial halls. Those spaces are for parking chariots and other vehicles. The leaders of the sacrifices, after completing the rituals in the sacrificial halls, mount their chariots parked there and depart. Similarly, the celestial vehicles and conveyances of the celestials who came to receive oblations are parked in those places. They, the celestials, receive the oblations and depart in their vehicles. The leaders of the sacrifices, after completing the sacrificial rituals, mount their chariots and come into the streets of Thiruchengalur and go to their respective homes. This implies that the leaders who performed sacrifices in those times were very wealthy. Now, Sekkizhar Peruman sings about the prosperity of Thiruchengalur. Red water lilies bloom in the irrigation channels. The fields are fertile, surrounded by water. There are bundles of red paddy stalks in the fields. Next to the fields are areca nut trees. Due to abundant water, the areca nut trees have bloomed with flower spathes. Lotus flowers with many leaves have bloomed there. Carp fish sleep in the large lotus flowers. Along the path between the fields, delicate jasmine creepers spread like a canopy. Kanchi trees with spreading branches stand on both sides of the street, allowing them to spread. Sekkizhar Peruman continues to sing about the glories of Thiruchengalur. Let us continue to reflect. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 05 — Chandesura Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periyapuranam, Part 249, Mummaiyaal Ulagaanda Sarukkam, Chandesura Nayanar Puranam. Sekkizhar Peruman continues to sing about the glories of Thiruchengalur. Thiruchengalur is one of the five cities where Chola kings were crowned. Chola kings traditionally crowned themselves in five cities within their country. These five cities are Kaveri Poompattinam, also known as Poompuhar, Thiruvarur, Uraiyur, Sengalur, and Karur. While singing about the Chola lineage that underwent such coronations, Sekkizhar Peruman sings that King Anapaya Chola was born into the Chola lineage, and Thiruchengalur is one of the five places where the Chola kings of that lineage, to which Anapaya Chola belonged, were crowned. Anapaya Chola was the king who commissioned Sekkizhar Peruman to compose Periyapuranam. Therefore, out of gratitude for commissioning this Puranam, Sekkizhar Peruman has praised Anapaya's devotion in eleven places in Periyapuranam. This place is one of the most significant of those eleven places. Anapaya Chola, also known as Abayan and Kulothunga Chola in the Chola lineage, ruled with great distinction. He was the Chola king who covered the sacred boundary of Thillai with gold. Sekkizhar Peruman sings that Thiruchengalur possesses the quality of being one of the five holy places where the ancient Chola kings, of the lineage in which the glorious Anapaya Chola was born, were crowned. Sekkizhar Peruman notes here that Anapaya was known by two names: Abayan and Kulothungan. He also mentions that he covered the sacred boundary of Thillai with gold. We have reflected on the story of Anapaya Chola requesting Sekkizhar Peruman to compose Periyapuranam in the introduction to Periyapuranam. Such was the glorious king Anapaya Chola. Sekkizhar Peruman refers to Anapaya here as Kulothunga Chola. Kulothunga Chola is a name adopted by many Chola kings. There have been many Chola kings with the name Kulothunga Chola. However, Anapaya is the special name of Anapaya Chola. That is why Sekkizhar Peruman has praised this king by using the name Anapaya in many places. Anapaya has many glories. The most prominent of all these glories is that he lived with great devotion to sacred ash. He covered the Thillai boundary with gold. These two virtues captivated Sekkizhar Peruman's heart. Therefore, he praises these two sacred services in many places. Anapaya Chola excelled in his devotion to sacred ash. Therefore, Anapaya also has the name Thiruneetru Chola (Chola of Sacred Ash). The Chola king who covered the great temple of Thillai, its temple and gopuram, with gold, the Thiruneetru Chola, covered it with gold for the flourishing of the Saivite path. This is mentioned in the Chidambara Puranam. The Koyil Puranam also states the same. Furthermore, Abayan and Kulothunga Chola are names borne by many Chola kings. But Anapayar is his special name. That is why Sekkizhar Peruman praises this king using the name Anapayar in many places. Moreover, in this verse, Sekkizhar Peruman refers to Anapaya Chola as "ennrum puvi kaakkum mannan" (the king who forever protects the earth). This conveys Sekkizhar Peruman's blessing that the reign of his beloved king may last long. It was at Anapaya Chola's request that Sekkizhar Peruman composed Thiruthondar Puranam, also known as Periyapuranam, and even today, the world thrives in the Saivite path because of this Thiruthondar Puranam. It would not be an exaggeration to say that this is due to the strength of Anapaya's rule, according to Saivite scholars. Having sung thus, Sekkizhar Peruman continues to sing about the glories of Thiruchengalur. It is a very beautiful verse. The benefit of a tune is good music. The benefit of milk is sweet taste. The benefit of having eyes is that they have light. The benefit of thought is the sacred five letters. The benefit of the sky is the rain it pours. The benefit of the Veda is Saivism. Similarly, what is the benefit of this earth? It is the holy place of Thiruchengalur. Can its greatness be measured? Sekkizhar Peruman sings that its greatness is immeasurable. The author has conveyed many great truths in this verse using similes. The author's teaching and imparting these truths for the benefit of the world are noteworthy and commendable in this verse. Now, the detailed meaning of this verse. First, he states that the benefit of a tune is good music. "Pann" refers to tunes like Kurinji Pann, Mullai Pann, Paalai Pann, etc. This is a musical arrangement unique to Tamil. What we now call "ragam" was called "pann" in Tamil in ancient times. "Pann" is said to be the creation of a melodious raga by appropriately placing the seven notes according to one's mental state. From the period of Tholkappiyam until the end of the Chola empire, Tamil music was entirely based on "pann." Texts state that for a song, its corresponding "pann" is essential. Our ancestors have established rules for the hymns in the Thevarams of the Three Saints, specifying which "pann" should be used for each hymn. According to this, even today, Oduvar Moorthigal (reciters) in all temples recite those hymns in their respective "panns" and offer them to the Lord. Furthermore, we have reflected on the information about "pann" in the Thirumalai Sarukkam, in the Thaduthaatkonda Puranam, where Sekkizhar Peruman states that Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sang the hymn "Piththa Piraisoodi" to the Lord in Indalappann. We can recall all those details here and now reflect on the meaning of Sekkizhar Peruman's statement, "Pannin payanaam nal isai" (The benefit of a tune is good music). Sekkizhar Peruman says, "Pannin payanaam nal isai." This means that a tune yields benefit only when it is accompanied by music. If there is no music, the tune will not yield any benefit. Music is a connection that includes the praise of God. We can also relate this to Thiruisaippa, which is found in the ninth Thirumurai. Music should be sung with objects that are full of God's praise. A mere tune without God's praise will only be a pleasant sound to the ears. At the same time, tunes, songs, and hymns that praise God will enter the mind, melt the mind and soul, and bestow upon us the sacred feet of God. Songs that do not praise God will only provide sensory pleasure, bringing both joy and sorrow to living beings. Songs that praise God will provide strength to the soul. This is what Appar Swamigal mentioned in Thiruppazhanam Thevaram, "Pann porundha isai paadum." Thirugnanasambandha Peruman said in Pullirukkuvelur Thiruppathigam, "Pann ondrai isai paadum adiyargal kudiyaga manninri vin kodukkum Manigandan" (Manigandan, who gives heaven without earth to the devotees who sing with a tune). Sundaramoorthy Swamigal mentioned this in Kurukkavur Thevaram as "Pannidai Tamil oppaay." Therefore, only music that is combined with the praise of God will yield true benefits. Mere music without the praise of God will only be sensory pleasure. This is the meaning of Sekkizhar Peruman's statement. Therefore, Sivakavimani Aiya says that those who engage in mere music without the praise of God and spend money and time on it should realize this. This is the meaning of "Pannin payanaam nallisai." Now, Sekkizhar Peruman sings, "Paalin payanaam insuvai" (The benefit of milk is sweet taste). What is the benefit of milk? He sings that it is sweet taste. In this Puranam, Chandesura Nayanar worships by anointing the Lord with milk milked from cows. This is how this story unfolded. Sekkizhar Swamigal implicitly suggests that this story resulted from the sweetness of the milk offered to the Lord for anointing, and thus states, "Paalin payan insuvai." Furthermore, in the Thiruvilaiyadal Puranam, in the chapter on carrying earth, it is said in the 23rd verse, "Annai mulai theempalin ariya suvai idhu" (This is the rare taste of mother's sweet breast milk). This verse provides another explanation for the benefit of milk being sweet taste. The Thiruvilaiyadal Puranam describes how the Vaigai river overflowed in Madurai, and the Pandyan king ordered one person from each house to block the banks of the Vaigai. When Vandippatti, a woman selling pittu (a sweet dish), lamented that it would be good to have a hired laborer, Lord Shiva himself came forward as a laborer for Vandippatti. The author of Thiruvilaiyadal Puranam sings that he came eagerly to eat the pittu that Vandippatti had. How did the Lord come? He came before Vandippatti with a spade on his sacred shoulder. Sages lovingly offer oblations in sacrifices to the Lord. Even after eating that, his hunger was not satisfied. Even after sweetly eating the nectar fed by Uma Devi, his hunger was not satisfied. Paranjothi Munivar sings in that verse that the Lord came to eat the pittu that Vandippatti was about to give, like a child eagerly coming to drink its mother's breast milk with unquenchable hunger. One phrase in that verse is "Annai mulai theempalin ariya suvai idhu." Therefore, the meaning that the benefit of milk is sweet taste is well understood through this verse as well. Now, Sekkizhar Peruman sings that the benefit of the eye is light. The eye here refers to the organ of sight. In the external world, there are great luminous objects like the sun. Nevertheless, the benefit for the eye is that there must be light for it to see. Having that light to see is what constitutes the benefit for the eye. Next, Sekkizhar Peruman sings that the benefit of thought is the sacred five letters. The conclusion of the Jnana Shastras is that the mind is given to humans only for contemplating the sacred five letters. "Karuththu" here means heart or mind. Humans are given a heart, mind, and consciousness. For what purpose? Only for remembering the sacred five letters, only for contemplating the sacred five letters. We can see this in the Thevaram, "Vaazhththa vaayum ninaikka madanenjum" (A mouth to praise and a foolish mind to remember). There are many other such sacred sayings. Remembering Lord Shiva in the mind is the benefit of having a mind. If one does not remember, it means that the benefit from the mind has not been obtained. The five letters are the sacred name of God. What does it mean to remember these sacred five letters with the mind? It means that the message conveyed to us through the sacred five letters is the great compassion that Lord Shiva bestows upon living beings. Therefore, the inherent message of Sri Panchaksharam is the great compassion that Lord Shiva bestows upon living beings. Meditating on such great compassion and melting with devotion is what it means to remember the sacred five letters. Now, he sings that the benefit of the sky is the rain it pours. The sky provides space for everything. Linguists say that it is called "akasam" because it provides "avakasam" (opportunity or space) for everything. Such a sky gives space for rain and causes it to pour. Therefore, causing rain to pour is considered the benefit of the sky or akasam. Regarding this, Thirukkural mentions in the chapter on the Glory of Rain, "Vaanindru ulagam vazhangi varuthalaal" (Because the world thrives from the sky) and "Visumbin thuli veezhin allaal" (Unless a drop falls from the sky). Now, Sekkizhar Peruman sings that the benefit of the Veda is Saivism. The ultimate meaning of the Veda is Saivism. Saivism refers to the meaning of Shiva Agamas. If we consider the meaning conveyed by the Shiva Agamas mentioned in the Atharva Shikha and other Upanishads, the Vedas are like sutras, and the Shiva Agamas are like commentaries. Following the Thoola Arundhati method (gross to subtle), the Vedas are called general texts, and the Agamas are called special texts. "Vedantha thelivaam siddhanthath thiran ingu therikkalutram" (Here we shall explain the essence of Siddhanta, which is the clarity of Vedanta) is from Sivaprakasam, preface seven. Therefore, if we consider all these truths from the Jnana Shastras, the benefit of the Veda being Saivism becomes clear to us. Madhava Sivagnana Yogigal has explained these facts in the special preface to Sivagnana Mapadiyam. Now, Sekkizhar Peruman sings that the benefit of the earth is Thiruchengalur. Earth here refers to the world made of earth. This is called "Prithvi Andam" (earthly sphere). The benefit of this earth is Thiruchengalur itself. If this holy place of Thiruchengalur did not exist, all the earthly worlds would be useless. Therefore, this holy place is what gives benefit to the earthly world. Unlike other higher and lower worlds, which are realms of enjoyment, this earthly world is a world where living beings are born, worship Lord Shiva, and attain liberation. This place is blessed with Chandeesa Nayanar, who helps living beings who are born here and perform Shiva Puja to attain the benefit of that Shiva Puja. That is why Sekkizhar Peruman sings that this place is the "mannin payanaam pathi" (the place that is the benefit of the earth). We saw in the first verse that Lord Muruga completed this path by worshipping Lord Shiva, showing that this is the meaning for all living beings in the world. Sekkizhar Peruman, by stating the ability to bestow the benefit of such Shiva Puja, sings, "Is there any other place equal to this prosperous place? Can its glories be described?" Thus, Sekkizhar Peruman sings, using six similes: the benefit of a tune is music, the benefit of milk is taste, the benefit of the eye is light, the benefit of thought is the sacred five letters, the benefit of the sky is rain, and the benefit of the Veda is Saivism, and then states that the benefit of the earth is this holy place of Sengalur. The inner meaning of this is that by singing with appropriate tunes, anointing the Lord with milk, seeing Him with one's eyes, remembering His sacred five letters with one's thoughts, worshipping Lord Shiva with cool holy water filled by rain from the sky, and performing Shiva Puja according to Vedic and Agamic texts, this place of Thiruchengalur possesses the glory of bestowing the benefit of that Shiva Agamic Puja upon the people of the earthly world. Let us continue to reflect. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 06 — Chandesura Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periyapuranam, Part 250. Mummaiyaal Ulaganda Sarukkam, Chandesura Nayanar Puranam. Sekkizhar Perumaan continues to sing the glories of Thiruchengalur. In this glorious Thiruchengalur, in the Brahmin lineage of the Kashyapa Gotra, lived a Brahmin named Echadattan. Among Brahmins, there are many sub-divisions called Gotras. Among them, the Kashyapa Gotra is connected to the sage Kashyapa. Echadattan was a Vedic scholar who lived in a small sub-division within such a Gotra. A small sub-division within a Gotra is called Pravaram. The name of that Brahmin was Echadattan. The word "Echam" in Tamil comes from "Yajnam" in Sanskrit. They came from a tradition of performing sacrifices, so the name "Echam" was given to them. "Dattan" was one of the special names adopted by Brahmins in ancient times. Therefore, his name was Echadattan. This Echadattan was one who embodied both good and bad karma. Just as a snake can yield both gems and poison, Echadattan was blessed with the good karma of begetting a virtuous son named Visarasarumar. At the same time, he was also destined to commit the evil deed of obstructing the Shiva Puja performed by Visarasarumar, which is a Shiva Aparatham (offense against Shiva). Therefore, Sekkizhar sings that Echadattan embodied both good and bad karma, and he uses the analogy of a snake. A snake has both gems and poison. Similarly, Sekkizhar Swamigal says that Echadattan lived. This Echadattan was engaged in domestic life and led an excellent household. The greatness of domestic life, according to Thirukkural, is "Nallaatrin nindra thunai" (a good support for the three natural stages of life) and "Thurandhaarkkum thuvvaathavarkkum irandhaarkkum thunaiyaavadhu" (a support for ascetics, the poor, and the deceased). It also involves protecting the five groups: "Thenpulathaar, Deivam, Virundhu, Okkal, Thaan" (ancestors, gods, guests, relatives, and oneself). These are the virtues of householders mentioned in Thirukkural. Our elders ordained that household life should not be for oneself, but for helping others. Echadattan had a virtuous wife named Pavithirai. She was born into a lineage suitable for marriage, in accordance with Echadattan's tradition. Earlier, when describing Echadattan, it was said that he embodied both good and bad karma. Here, when describing his wife, Pavithirai, Sekkizhar Perumaan sings that she was not in such a dual state, but stood in a single state, supporting her husband in attaining Shiva's feet. For a woman, the excellence of her birthplace, lineage, and qualities is very important. Pavithirai was born into such a distinguished and virtuous lineage. In her household life, she loved, protected, and nurtured all her relatives. Nurturing relatives is a great duty and profession for a wife in domestic life. This is a special quality a woman should possess in her marital home. Such was Pavithirai. Pavithirai means "one who is pure." Pavithiram means "purity." Therefore, Pavithirai has the meaning of "one who is pure" and also "one who has the quality of purifying living beings." Thus, Echadattan and Pavithirai lived their domestic life. Pavithirai was blessed with the penance of begetting a virtuous son. Begetting virtuous sons is the penance of wives in domestic life.

It is beneficial. Thirukkural states, "Mangalam enba mana maatchi matru adhan nankalam nanmakkal perru" (A good wife is a blessing, and her best ornament is virtuous children). Raising virtuous children is the fruit of penance. Those who do not beget and raise good children do not attain the benefit of household life. What is a good son? He is one who not only helps his parents prosper in this world but also helps them avoid hell in the afterlife. Such a one is a good son. But here, Pavithirai is about to give birth to our Chandesura Nayanar, Visarasarumar. Our Nayanar, who came as their son, not only bestowed good upon his parents in this world and the next but also helped them attain the ultimate world of liberation. Pavithirai is the one who possesses such penance to beget such a Nayanar. Pavithirai, the woman, is about to attain the ultimate benefit of liberation, which is the greatest of all blessings attained by those who beget sons in the world, through the son she is about to bear. She had performed penance before to beget Visarasarumar, who is Chandesura Nayanar, as her son. The fruit of all the penances she performed in previous births is to be realized in this birth by begetting Visarasarumar as her son. Such a Pavithirai is one who has abandoned all other attachments and holds only to the attachment to Shiva's feet. Thirukkural states, "Patruka patratraan patrinai appatrai patruka patru vidarku" (Cling to the attachment of the one who is unattached; cling to that attachment to shed all other attachments). Attachment is the anava mala (ego impurity). It afflicts the soul from eternity, causing suffering without release. Pavithirai was one who held such an attachment to God, causing the great power of anava mala to lose its strength. Gnana Sastram (scriptures of wisdom) states that love for God weakens anava mala. Thus, Pavithirai is one who holds firmly to Shiva's feet. By giving birth to a virtuous son, Pavithirai is a firm support for her husband. She also stands in Shiva's discipline, which helps her attain such attachment. Many indications are present here: that by the birth of her son, Chandesura Nayanar, she also provides the support to other beings to develop attachment to Shiva and shed the attachment of anava. The implication here is that through Pavithirai's attachment to Shiva, beings will always be able to shed anava attachment and attain Shiva attachment. Thus, Chandesura Nayanar, also known as Visarasarumar, takes his sacred birth to Echadattan and Pavithirai, who perform such outward good deeds. Visarasarumar takes his sacred birth here so that the paths of beneficial Vedas may flourish, the lineage of Vedic scholars may ever increase, the seven worlds may be saved, the truth of Saivism of the Lord who performs the sacred dance in the Thiruchitrambalam may grow, and the victory of great ascetics may shine. He is destined to attain the position of Chandesa. Sekkizhar Perumaan sings in the following verse that Visarasarumar takes his sacred birth to bestow all these benefits upon living beings: "Nandri puriyum avar thampaal nanmai maraiyin thurai vilanga endrum maraiyor kulam peruga ezhu puvanangalum uyya mandril nadanam seybavar saiva vaaimai valara maathavaththor venri vilanga vandhu udhayam seidhaar Visarasarumar" (Visarasarumar was born to make the paths of beneficial Vedas flourish, to ever increase the lineage of Vedic scholars, to save the seven worlds, to make the truth of Saivism of the Lord who dances in the assembly grow, and to make the victory of great ascetics shine). It is a rule of Shiva temple worship that one must worship Chandesura Nayanar in the Shiva temple and complete the Shiva temple worship. Chandesura Nayanar is the one who bestows the benefit of Shiva temple worship and Shiva Puja. Therefore, he takes his sacred birth here to bestow the benefit of Shiva Puja, which is the penance performed by all living beings.

Sekkizhar Perumaan thus praises Chandesura Nayanar, also known as Visarasarumar, who performed the sacred acts, and sings about his sacred birth. The first benefit that came from Visarasarumar's sacred birth, Sekkizhar sings, is "good paths of the Vedas may flourish." What is the benefit of the Vedas? It is to know and follow the rules stated in the Vedas, and to know and avoid what is prohibited. By doing so, the world will be guided on the right path. Thus, the beneficial Vedas flourish. Sekkizhar Perumaan sings "Vedathin thurai vilanga" (the paths of the Vedas may flourish). This means that the principles stated in the Vedas become clear through the life story of Visarasarumar. The principles stated in the Vedas include the state of Brahmacharya, Upanayanam, studying the Vedas, performing Vedic rituals with samit (sacred wood) and fire, protecting cows, and other Vedic principles. All these will remain eternal in this world because of Visarasarumar's sacred birth. Next, Sekkizhar Perumaan sings, "andrum maraiyavar kulam peruga" (and the lineage of Vedic scholars may ever increase). Visarasarumar took his sacred birth in the lineage of Brahmins, protected cows, performed Shiva Puja, and was accepted as Shiva's son. Knowing and following this historical event, all Brahmins of that lineage worship Shiva and prosper. Therefore, Sekkizhar Perumaan sings that Visarasarumar took his sacred birth for the prosperity of the Brahmin lineage. Next, he sings that Visarasarumar was born to save the seven worlds. The conclusion of the scriptures of wisdom is that salvation for living beings comes only through performing Shiva Puja and its benefits. Nothing else can bring salvation to living beings. They will attain the path of Saivism. Shivagnana Siddhiyar states that after practicing Chariya, Kriya, and Yoga, one attains Shiva's feet through Gnana (wisdom). Through the sacred birth of this Nayanar, the world will be saved. How? By following the path shown by him, performing Shiva Puja, and receiving its benefits from him, this world will be saved. Therefore, Sekkizhar Perumaan sings, "ezhu puvanangalum uyya" (to save the seven worlds). Next, he sings, "saiva vaaimai valara" (the truth of Saivism may grow). What does it mean for the truth of Saivism to grow? It means that the established truth that Shiva Puja, as taught in Saivism, is what truly benefits people, is spread by him. Therefore, Sekkizhar Perumaan sings that the truth of Saivism grows. Next, he sings, "maathavaththor venri vilanga" (the victory of great ascetics may shine). This means that the victory of those who perform Shiva Puja becomes clear. "Maathavam" means Shiva Puja. Among all penances, Shiva Puja is the foremost. Therefore, Shiva Puja is the great penance. He says that this great penance, Shiva Puja, should be victorious. What does that mean? It means that no matter what obstacles come to Shiva Puja, those obstacles will not hinder Shiva Puja, and the ascetics will continue to perform Shiva Puja, and all obstacles to Shiva Puja will vanish before it. This is the meaning of Sekkizhar Perumaan's "maathavaththor venri vilanga." Even in the story of our Nayanar, the act of the Nayanar killing his father, a Brahmin, ultimately leads to good and is seen as a victory that leads to liberation. Even though this is considered a great sin by worldly people, it turns into good and leads to liberation. Such a victory is the victory of Shiva Puja. Thus, Sekkizhar Perumaan sings that he was born for all these benefits to be attained by living beings. Just as the sun, moon, and other luminous objects rise and dispel the outer darkness of the world, so too Chandesura Nayanar rises here. Therefore, the darkness of anava (ego) will be dispelled, and living beings will attain liberation. This is the benefit of the Nayanar's sacred birth. Thus, Chandesura Nayanar takes his birth. He is given the name Visarasarumar. Visarasarumar is the child's sacred name. The Nayanar's child's sacred name. Sarumar is a traditional name for Vedic scholars.

The name "Sarma" in Sanskrit is rendered as "Sarumar" in Tamil. Therefore, his name is called Visara Sarma in Sanskrit and Visarasarumar in Tamil. The word "Visaram" here means "investigation into the nature of the soul." Our Chandesura Nayanar, in all his previous births, had investigated the nature of the soul, and with that matured understanding, he was born here as a Samusiddhar. A Samusiddhar is one who can bestow grace upon souls like Pralayakalar and Vignanakalar. Shivagnana Siddhiyar describes Samusiddhars as "Thondar" (devotees) who appear through ancient good penance and worship the Supreme Being. This is the meaning of the word "Visaram." What is "investigation"? It is to investigate and understand the existence of souls, the nature of the bonds that bind them, and the God who removes those bonds and bestows joy, without doubt or error, and to follow the path of Saiva sadhanas to remove those bonds. This is called "investigation" or "visaram." Thus, the meaning of the Nayanar's child's sacred name, Visarasarumar, is established. Thus, Visarasarumar took his sacred birth to make the paths of beneficial Vedas flourish, to ever increase the lineage of Vedic scholars, to save the seven worlds, to make the truth of Saivism grow, to make the victory of Shiva Puja performed by great ascetics shine, for living beings to perform Shiva Puja, and to bestow the benefits of that Shiva Puja, and to attain the position of Chandesa. Chandesura Nayanar is capable of bestowing the benefits of Shiva Puja performed by all souls so far. From now on, Chandesura Nayanar is the one who prevents and protects against obstacles to Shiva Puja performed by souls. Such a Nayanar, Chandesura Nayanar, takes his sacred birth. Therefore, it is certain that the benefits of the Shiva Puja we have performed so far will be realized, and no obstacles will come to the Shiva Puja we perform from now on. Let us continue to contemplate the Puranam of Chandesura Nayanar. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 07 — Chandesura Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periyapuranam, Part 251. Mummaiyaal Ulaganda Sarukkam, Chandesura Nayanar Puranam. Chandesura Nayanar takes his sacred birth in Thiruchengalur with the child's sacred name Visarasarumar. As he grew, he completed five years of age. At that time, the Nayanar had already lived according to the Shivagamas that Lord Shiva had previously revealed in all his previous births. Due to the continuity of that Shivagnana, a mature understanding arose in the Nayanar. The Vedas are composed of Chandas (metrical hymns) with six angas (auxiliary disciplines). Lord Shiva, the head of the Vedas, revealed the Agamas. That understanding of the Agamas shone brightly in the Nayanar when he completed five years of age, with a mature understanding. In this verse, Sekkizhar Perumaan sings that the Agamas were revealed by Lord Shiva, the head of the Vedas. For Saivites, the Vedas are general scriptures, and the Agamas are special scriptures. The Vedas were revealed by Lord Shiva for worldly people, and the Agamas for those who have attained Shakti Nipaatham (descent of divine grace). It is implicitly conveyed here that those who study the Agamas must first study the Vedas and their angas. It is also conveyed by Sekkizhar Perumaan that the Vedas are subordinate to the Agamas, and that the Agamas contain the hidden meanings of the Vedas and reveal the ultimate truths that lie beyond them. Thirumoolar Perumaan's Thirumandiram states, "Vedamodu Agamam meyyaam Iraivan nool Odhum podhuvum sirappum endru unnuga" (Consider the Vedas and Agamas as the true scriptures of God, both general and special). In the initial stages, souls, according to their maturity and karma, will be born and wander in external and external-internal religions, such as Lokayatham. They will be born and wander in external and external-internal religions without realizing the ultimate truth stated in internal religions such as Saivism. Then, gradually, God will bestow grace upon souls, causing them to mature through external religions, external-internal religions, and internal religions, and accordingly attain births. Such souls, after being born in the other religions mentioned above, will then, without fail, stand in the four stages of Brahmacharya, Grihastha, Vanaprastha, and Sannyasa, prescribed for the four varnas of Brahmins, Kshatriyas, Vaishyas, and Shudras. They will perform many yagnas systematically according to Shruti and Smriti, and then mature by investigating the meaning of Vedanta without doubt or error, like a gooseberry in the palm of one's hand. After all these are completed, the desire to attain moksha will increase. Such souls are eligible for Samaya Diksha (initiation into a religious path), which is specifically mentioned in the Saiva religion, which clearly explains the three entities: Pati (Lord), Pasu (soul), and Pasam (bondage). Souls who are eligible for Samaya Diksha and have received Samaya Diksha from Saiva Acharyas according to the Saiva Agama will perform the following actions prescribed for Samaya Diksha. The actions prescribed for Samaya Diksha are as follows: sweeping and cleaning the temples where God resides, anointing with cow dung, plucking flowers with devotion and stringing them into various garlands, lighting lamps, and serving the devotees of Shiva, which is said to be the purpose of taking this body, and thereby receiving the grace of those Shiva devotees. Souls who have received Samaya Diksha will perform all these actions. Souls who practice the Chariya path, also known as Dasa Margam (path of servitude), will attain Saloka Padavi (residence in the divine world). Then, after completing this Chariya path, the Dasa Margam, they will become eligible for Visesha Diksha (special initiation). Those who have received Visesha Diksha from Saiva Acharyas will meditate on Vidya Deha Murthy (the form of knowledge) and the pure Shiva, who is the embodiment of the form, invoke them, perform ornaments puja, and stand in the Kriya path, also known as Putra Margam (path of the son), and perform such Shiva Pujas without fail. Those who have received such Visesha Diksha and performed such Shiva Pujas will attain Samipya Padavi (proximity to God). Those who have attained the Putra Margam, which is the Kriya path, will thus attain Samipya Padavi, and then they will become eligible for Nirvana Diksha (initiation into liberation) and perform Shiva Yoga with eight angas. Those who have accomplished this Yoga path, also known as Sakha Margam (path of friendship), will live attaining Sarupya Padavi (likeness to God). Thus, those who have systematically completed Chariya, Kriya, and Yoga, and attained Salokya, Samipya, and Sarupya Padavi, will, as a result, be guided by God in the Gnana path (path of wisdom). Souls who have come to the Gnana path will, by the grace of God, understand the nature of the five bonds and their general characteristics, and by removing them, they will understand and contemplate these through special characteristics without doubt or error, and attain Samadhi. This is called Sanmargam (the good path) or Gnana Padam. Thus, through the wisdom bestowed by God, they will destroy the bonds, attain the great wealth of Shivananda (bliss of Shiva), and shine as Gnanis (wise ones). This wisdom is called Shivagnana or Pati Gnana. Sekkizhar Perumaan sings about this consciousness, Shivagnana, and says that such Shivagnana manifested in the Nayanar when he completed five years of age. He sings this in this verse: "Mundhai arivin thodarchiyinaal mukaitha malarin vaasam pol Sindhai malara udan malarum sevvi unarvu sirandhadhaal" (Due to the continuity of previous knowledge, like the fragrance of a blooming flower, the mind blossomed, and with it, the mature consciousness shone brightly). That is, the Nayanar attained the Shivagnana mentioned in the Shivagamas due to the continuity of knowledge from previous births, when he completed five years of age. Just as the fragrance that appears when a bud blossoms, the mature consciousness shone brightly as the Nayanar's mind blossomed. Sekkizhar Perumaan sings that this mature consciousness shone brightly. This mature consciousness, Shivagnana, came or shone brightly due to the continuity of the knowledge the Nayanar had gained in his previous births. It is our tradition that the knowledge of previous births continues with the soul in this birth. Thirukkural states, "Orumaikkan thaan katra kalvi oruvarkku ezhumaiyum emaappu udaiththu" (The knowledge one acquires in one birth is a protection for seven births). Even if the body perishes, the soul, which is the essence of knowledge, does not perish. The knowledge it has acquired continues through many births. For this reason, there are differences in intelligence among people. Sekkizhar Perumaan sings here that the knowledge of the Agamas developed and manifested due to previous impressions. He says that it comes like the fragrance of a blooming flower. The fragrance of a flower appears when it blossoms. When the flower is a bud, there is no fragrance. But when it is a bud, its fragrance is subtle. When the same bud blossoms, the subtle fragrance manifests grossly. Therefore, if fragrance comes when a flower blossoms, it is because that fragrance was already subtly present in the bud and then manifests. "Ulladhu thondraathu illadhu vaaraathu" (What exists does not appear, what does not exist does not come) is the Satkaryavada (doctrine of pre-existence of effect in cause). What never existed will never appear. If something appears, it must have already existed. This means that only what exists will appear. Therefore, when a flower blossoms,

If fragrance comes, it is the Siddhanta (established truth) that it was present in a subtle state when it was a bud and then manifests grossly when it blossoms. Just as the fragrance emerges as the bud matures and blossoms, so too the previous continuity of Shivagnana now matures and manifests in our Nayanar. Sekkizhar Perumaan sings this as "Sindhai malara udan malarum sevvi unarvu" (the mature consciousness that blossoms with the blossoming of the mind). "Sindhai" is one of the four antakaranas (inner instruments). "Sindhai malara udan malardhal" means that as the mind expands, the knowledge that was associated with it, which was subtly contained before, expands. "Sevvi" means maturity, that is, the right time. This consciousness is called Shivagnana. Shivagnana is the means to the manifestation of Shivattva (the state of Shiva), as the knowledge filled with the consciousness of the Agamas, including the Vedas, blossoms. The idea here is that Shivagnana manifested in the Nayanar. Thirumandiram's verse, "Pooviniyil gandham porundhiyavaaru pola Jeevanukku ullae Shivamanam poothathu" (Just as fragrance pervades a flower, so too the Shiva fragrance blossomed within the soul), states this. This is the third verse in the seventh section, "Yoga," of the fifth Tantra of Thirumandiram. Thus, due to the continuity of Shivagnana that Chandesura Nayanar had acquired in his previous births, it now manifested brightly in the Nayanar when he completed five years of age. Generally, the age of five years is the age when one's consciousness begins to manifest, which is a common truth. Sekkizhar Perumaan has interpreted "Pirandhu mozhi payindra pin ellaam" (After being born and learning language) in Arputha Thiruvandhadhi as "Pirandhu unarvu thodangiya pin" (After being born and consciousness began). We will see this in the Puranam of Karaikkal Ammaiyar. Following this, the proverb "Aindhil valaiyaadhadhu aimbadhil valaiyumaa?" (Will what does not bend at five bend at fifty?) also takes shape. While this was happening, the Nayanar reached the age of seven. As we saw earlier, Shivagnana matured and manifested brightly in the Nayanar immediately after he completed five years of age. Nevertheless, Visarasarumar's parents performed the Upanayanam ceremony according to their family tradition. Upanayanam is a special ceremony for the first three varnas, starting with Brahmins. This Upanayanam ceremony is to be performed around the age of seven. The Upanayanam ceremony involves placing five tufts of hair on the child, adorning him with a deer skin and a sacred thread, initiating him with the Brahma mantra, and establishing him in the first stage of Brahmacharya. By performing this Upanayanam ceremony, the child attains the qualification of "Dvijar" (twice-born) and becomes eligible for Vedic study. "Upanayanam" literally means "another eye that has joined." "Upa" means "to approach," and "Nayana" means "to lead." It also means "to lead one to approach God." We saw that "Upanayanam" literally means "another eye that has joined." What does this mean? It means that the eye of wisdom is opened through the mantra initiation. Elders say that wisdom, which is the cause of the manifestation of Shivattva, is the third eye. The deer skin, the sacred thread, and the five tufts of hair, which are the symbols of Brahmacharya, are also based on this idea. Shiva is Brahman. One who knows Shiva must live bearing the symbols of Shiva. This is the meaning. Since Lord Shiva has five faces, the five tufts of hair worn by Brahmacharis is one of the symbols of Brahmacharya. The third eye belongs to Shiva. We have seen all these explanations in the history of Sundaramurthy Swamigal, in the Puranam of Thaduthatkondar. Thus, the Upanayanam ceremony is performed based on the idea that Shiva is Brahman and that one who knows Shiva must live bearing his symbols, as explained by Shivakavimani Ayya. Therefore, those who follow the Saiva tradition

Chapter 08 — Chandesura Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periyapuranam, Part 252. Mummaiyaal Ulaganda Sarukkam, Chandesa Nayanar Puranam. Visarasarumar, also known as Chandesa Nayanar, has his Upanayanam performed by his parents according to their family tradition. Visarasarumar, who is full of Shivagnana, begins his Vedic studies. The Vedas are the cause of the manifestation of wisdom. Our Visarasarumar, however, was already filled with that Shivagnana even before teachers instructed him in the Vedas and all other arts. This happened to the Lord as a continuation of the penance and Shiva Puja he performed in his previous births. Thus, Visarasarumar was so full of Shivagnana that he astonished the Brahmin teachers who taught the Vedas. Visarasarumar firmly held in his mind that the sacred feet of Lord Shiva are the ultimate limit of all things signified by all arts and all scriptures. Whatever the art, it will determine a certain object. Thus, all objects determined by all arts ultimately end at the sacred feet of the Lord. What does "ultimate limit" mean? It means the place where all arts converge and beyond which nothing more can be known, a place of rest. That is the ultimate limit, and those are the sacred feet of the Lord. We have seen the explanation of this in the tenth verse of Thiruvempavai: "Paadhaalam ezhinum keezh sorkazhivu paadhamalar podhaar punai mudiyum ellaap porul mudive" (Below the seven netherworlds, the sacred lotus feet beyond words, and the flower-adorned crown, are the ultimate end of all things). Visarasarumar, also known as Chandesa Nayanar, stands firm and clear with this wisdom in his mind. Therefore, he is so learned that he knows everything without being taught, to the astonishment of his teachers, the Brahmins who teach him the Vedas. Sekkizhar Perumaan praises the Nayanar in this verse with an oxymoron, calling him "Siria Perundhagaiyaar" (the small great one). "Siria" in "Siria Perundhagaiyaar" means that Visarasarumar is small in age and stature, a young child. "Perundhagaimai" (greatness) refers to the nobility of the Nayanar's heart. Therefore, Sekkizhar Perumaan calls Visarasarumar "Siria Perundhagaiyaar." Similarly, in the 73rd verse of the Thirugnanasambandha Perumaan Nayanar Puranam, he is referred to as "Ach siria Perundhagaiyaar." Visarasarumar realized the truth that Lord Shiva is our Lord. His love overflows every time he thinks of that truth. Through that love, a natural, effortless affection arises and grows ever stronger. Lord Shiva performs the dance of grace, the dance of bliss. The raised foot in that dance is the left foot. That foot bestows liberation. Therefore, Visarasarumar loves Lord Shiva, thinking of him as "our Lord" who possesses that foot that bestows liberation. The phrase "our Lord" reveals to us the nature of Shiva as Pati (Lord). Lord Shiva alone is Pati. All living beings are his servants. The bond that prevents living beings from attaining their Lord, Shiva, and experiencing bliss, is Pasam (bondage). The grace of God is capable of destroying the power of that Pasam and bestowing the bliss of liberation. Visarasarumar loves Lord Shiva, thinking of him as "our Lord" who possesses that raised foot that bestows such grace. Thus, when he contemplates the Lord's compassion, his love flows out incessantly. This love that Visarasarumar has for God is a continuous, unbroken love inherited from his previous births. As this love grew stronger in his mind, his mind became firmly fixed on God.

He stands firm in that steadfastness. One day, as Visarasarumar walked through the streets of Thiruchengalur with a group of students chanting the Vedas, he saw something. We have already seen that the schools for Vedic study and the sacrificial halls were outside the village, and that groups of Vedic scholars would leave the village at the appointed time to study the Vedas, passing through the village streets. As the students studying the Vedas walked through the streets in the morning, at the same time, cowherds were also driving the village's herds of cows to graze. After giving milk at their homes in the morning, the cows would be led to the grazing lands on the banks of the Manniyar River near Thiruchengalur. So, the group of students chanting the Vedas was walking down the street. At the same time, cowherds were leading the cows for grazing. At that moment, a cow that had recently calved was reluctant to go grazing. This was because the cow was unwilling to leave its calf at home and go grazing. So, the cow shook its head at the cowherd who was leading it to graze, indicating that it would not come for grazing, that it could not leave its calf. The cowherd insisted on taking it to graze. So, the cow shook its head and tried to butt the cowherd with its horns. As the cow tried to butt him with its horns, the cowherd, without any hesitation, took a stick and beat the cow. Our Visarasarumar, who was walking in the group chanting the Vedas, saw this. He felt great love for the cow and anger towards the cowherd, and he stopped his action. Our Visarasarumar stopped the cowherd who was beating the cow. Visarasarumar knew the greatness of cows. He had a clear and discerning mind that understood the truth spoken by all arts, all scriptures, the vast collection of Agamic texts, and the glorious Vedas. He knew how the greatness of cows was praised in the Vedas, Agamas, and other scriptures. Visarasarumar knew how the Vedas and Agamas spoke of the greatness of cows. This is because Visarasarumar had attained Shivagnana, the ultimate conclusion of the Agamas. Visarasarumar possessed a mind with a clear understanding of the states of Pati (Lord), Pasu (soul), Pasam (bondage), and Arul (grace). Therefore, he truly knew the greatness of cows. That is why he stopped the cowherd from beating the cow and bestowed grace upon the cowherd. Visarasarumar, out of compassion, instructed the cowherd about the greatness of cows. In the following verse composed by Sekkizhar, Visarasarumar instructs the cowherd about the greatness of cows: "Thangum akila yonigatkum melaam perumai thagaimaiyena Pongu punitha theerthangal ellaam endrum porundhuvana Thunga amarar thirumunivar ganangal soozhndhu piriyadha Angam anaithum thaam udaiyavallavo nal aaninangal" (Are not these good cattle endowed with a greatness superior to all existing species, and do they not always contain all the overflowing sacred waters, and are not all their limbs surrounded by and inseparable from the glorious gods and great sages?). In this verse, Sekkizhar Perumaan describes Visarasarumar instructing the cowherd about how cows are special and great. Its meaning is as follows: These herds of cows possess a greatness superior to all species. Cows contain all the overflowing pure sacred waters.

The great and glorious gods and groups of sages surround and bestow grace while residing in the cow. Visarasarumar asks the cowherd, "Are not these herds of cows endowed with the greatness of having groups of sages and gods residing in their limbs?" First, he says that herds of cows possess a greatness superior to all species. When living beings take birth in this world, the places they reside in are called species. Among all types of species, cows are the most glorious. Species refers to the type of birth one takes. Scriptures state that there are 8.4 million species. Among these 8.4 million species, the birth of a cow is the highest, which is a truth stated in our scriptures. Next, he states the greatness that all sacred waters reside in the cow. The seven great sacred rivers like Ganga and Yamuna, and the Maha Maga Theertham that purifies these seven great sacred rivers, and countless other such sacred waters are contained within the cow. Furthermore, our scriptures state that great gods like Brahma, Vishnu, Varuna, Agni, Shiva ganas, other gods, and sages all reside in each limb of the cow. Therefore, herds of cows are born to benefit living beings. We saw that all sacred waters and gods reside in the body of a cow. The Shiva Dharmottaram explains this: Brahma and Vishnu reside at the base of the cow's horns. The sacred rivers like Godavari and all movable and immovable beings reside at the tip of the cow's horns. Lord Shiva resides in the cow's head. Uma Devi resides in the middle of its forehead. Lord Muruga resides in the cow's upper nostril. Lord Ganesha resides in the cow's inner nostril. The Ashwini Devas reside in both ears of the cow. Surya and Chandra reside in both eyes of the cow. Vayu resides in the cow's teeth. Varuna resides in the cow's tongue. The cow's chest, which possesses the exalted Omkara, is where Saraswati, the goddess of learning, resides. Yama resides in the heart. Yakshas reside in the temples. The presiding deities of the three times—sunrise, noon, and sunset—reside on the cow's lips. Indra resides in the cow's neck. Arukkan resides in the cow's hump. Saathiyar resides in the chest. Anila Vayu resides in the cow's four legs. The Maruts reside in the cow's knees. The Nagaloka dwellers reside in the cow's hooves. Gandharvas reside in the middle of the hooves. Deva Madars (divine women) reside on the upper part of the hooves. Rudras reside on the cow's back. The Ashtavasus reside in the joints. The Pitru Devas (ancestral deities) reside in the cow's waist. The Sapta Madars (seven mothers) reside in the cow's vagina. Mahalakshmi, the goddess of wealth, resides in the anus. The Devas reside at the base of the tail. The light of the sun resides in the cow's tail hair. Thus, the Devas reside in the limbs of the cow. Now, let's see how the sacred waters reside in the limbs of the cow. The celestial Ganga resides in the cow's urine. The Yamuna resides in the cow's dung. Bhumidevi (Mother Earth) resides in the cow's stomach. The great oceans reside in the cow's udder. The three fires—Garhapatya, Ahavaneeya, and Dakshinagni—reside in the cow's stomach, heart, and mouth. The ascetics reside in the cow's hair. All sacrificial activities reside in the cow's bones and semen. Chaste women reside in all the cow's limbs. Thus, Sekkizhar Perumaan, incorporating all these Shiva Dharmottaram facts, describes the greatness of cows in this verse.

Sekkizhar Perumaan has arranged for Visarasarumar to instruct the cowherd about its greatness. We will continue to contemplate. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 09 — Chandesura Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periyapuranam, Part 253. Mummaiyaal Ulaganda Sarukkam, Chandesura Nayanar Puranam. We saw the verse where Visarasarumar, also known as Chandesa Nayanar, instructs the cowherd about the greatness of cows. We also saw all the greatness of cows. Furthermore, Sekkizhar Perumaan sings that Visarasarumar states that cows alone have the right to provide the Panchagavya necessary for the sacred ablution of our Lord Shiva, who dances in the Thiruchitrambalam, on His sacred crown. We first saw how cows possess a pure body. Such pure-bodied cows alone have the right to provide the Panchagavya for the Lord's sacred ablution. Panchagavya consists of cow ghee, milk, curd, cow dung, and cow urine. We have contemplated the details of these in the Puranam of Arivattaya Nayanar. Cows alone have the right to provide such Panchagavya. This right has been granted to cows by God. Visarasarumar instructs that herds of cows possess such greatness. Not only that, Visarasarumar also states that these cows are the very forms that provide the cow dung, called Gomayam, which is the raw material for the sacred ash (Thiruneeru) that Lord Shiva adorns. Lord Shiva is the prime cause who protects all gods and all worlds throughout time. The epithet "prime cause" here means that Lord Shiva is the ultimate cause who has no superior. He is capable of creating all protecting deities, protecting them, and finally absorbing them into Himself. Therefore, he is referred to as the "protecting prime cause" in this verse by Sekkizhar Perumaan. Thiruvachagam states, "Padaipon padaikkum pazhaiyavan kaappon kaakkum kadavul" (The creator creates, the ancient one protects, the protecting God). The sacred ash that such a Lord Shiva adorns is imperishable even at the time of ultimate dissolution, because it resides on the Lord's sacred body. Even during the Mahasamhara (great dissolution), Thiruneeru is imperishable. The Thiruneeru mentioned here is what devotees use to adorn the Lord's sacred body. The cow dung, called Gomayam, is the source for obtaining this Thiruneeru. And cows are the source from which such Gomayam is produced, are they not? Therefore, Visarasarumar states that these cows, which benefit living beings, are the abode of all gods, sages, and sacred waters, provide the Panchagavya necessary for the Lord's sacred ablution, and provide Gomayam, the raw material for the sacred ash that adorns the Lord's sacred body. He further states that Lord Shiva, along with the Divine Mother, grants divine vision while seated on the Rishabha (divine bull). And is not this cow lineage the lineage of that Rishabha Devar? asks Visarasarumar. Lord Shiva holds a leaping deer in the tips of the fingers of His sacred hand. The deer calf, which came to destroy this world from the Abhichara Yagna performed by the Rishis, dances on the tips of the two fingers of Lord Shiva's left hand. We can see this story in detail in the Skanda Puranam. Lord Shiva, who holds such a leaping deer in His sacred hand, also has a glorious matted hair that holds the Ganga water, which contains the jewels of snakes. He, along with Uma Devi, the Empress of the Devas, is seated on the Rishabha. Here, Sekkizhar Perumaan refers to Uma Devi as "Devargal Thampiraatti" (Empress of the Devas). This means that Umaiyamman is the leader and queen of all the Devas. "Thampiraan" refers to Lord Shiva.

The feminine form of the word "Thampiraan" is "Thampiraatti." This word "Thampiraatti" is still in use in Malayalam country today. It is an ancient Tamil usage. Thus, Lord Shiva and Uma Devi, the Thampiraan and Thampiraatti, grant divine vision while seated on Rishabha Devar. Visarasarumar asks, "Is not this cow lineage the lineage of such a glorious Rishabha Devar?" Why has Sekkizhar arranged for Visarasarumar to say this here? It is because herds of cows exist on this earth for the salvation of souls. How did they come to this earth? They came to earth by the grace of Lord Shiva to help souls. Their history is as follows: In Shivaloka, Rishabha Devar resides in the presence of Lord Shiva. Beside Rishabha Devar are five types of cows: Nandai, Pathirai, Surabhi, Susilai, and Sumani. Among them, Nandai is of Kapil (brownish-yellow) color. Subathirai is of black color. Surabhi is of white color. Susilai is of smoke color. Sumani is of red color. Lord Shiva, by His grace, ordained that these five types of cows should go from Shivaloka to Bhuloka (earth) so that living beings on earth could perform Shiva Pujas, and so that the Panchagavya and Thiruneeru necessary for those Shiva Pujas would be available to living beings. Therefore, these five types of cows first appear in Thiruppaarkadal (the ocean of milk) by the grace of Lord Shiva. Then, they come from Thiruppaarkadal to Bhuloka. They all come to Bhuloka to assist in Shiva Pujas. This is the history of how herds of cows came to Bhuloka. Thus, these herds of cows came through the five types of cows that surround Rishabha Devar, on whom Lord Shiva and Uma Devi are seated. Therefore, Visarasarumar bestows grace and instructs, "Is not this cow lineage the lineage of Rishabha Devar, on whom the Lord and the Goddess are seated?" Having thus instructed, he says that the duty of grazing and protecting cows with their calves, so that they may be happy, is the most supreme duty. There is no other duty to be performed in this world above this duty. This is also the way to praise the sacred feet of Lord Shiva. He tells the cowherd, "From now on, you should give up the profession of grazing cows. I will graze these herds of cows myself." There are many duties that people must perform. Among all those duties, protecting cows is a great duty. It not only involves protecting cows but also becomes a way to praise the sacred feet of God. People have many duties. However, not all of them are ways to serve the sacred feet of Lord Shiva. Other duties are worldly and pertain to this body, and they will pass away. But the duty of protecting cows is not only the foremost among people's duties but also a way to worship the sacred feet of Lord Shiva. This is because cows provide all the materials needed for yagnas performed for Lord Shiva. Therefore, protecting cows becomes a Shiva Punya (meritorious act) that provides the means for Shiva sacrifices. Lord Shiva delights in Panchagavya and Thiruneeru. Therefore, protecting those exalted cows that provide Panchagavya and Thiruneeru, which are necessary for Lord Shiva's sacred ablution, becomes a Shiva Punya. Furthermore, Lord Shiva loves herds of cows. Therefore, Shiva will be pleased with those who protect cows. He will bestow grace. Therefore, protecting cows is also a way to please Lord Shiva. Among the ways to praise Lord Shiva, Shiva Archana (worship of Shiva) and yagna are excellent. Thus, protecting cows, which provide the means for Shiva Puja and Shiva yagna, is the best path. It is also the best duty. Having said all this, he tells the cowherd,

"From now on, I will graze these cows. You no longer need to graze them." He dismisses him. Hearing this, the cowherd was frightened and bowed to Visarasarumar, abandoning the profession of cow grazing. Thiruchengalur was a village inhabited by Brahmins. They were the leaders in that village. Visarasarumar was one of those Brahmins. Therefore, the cowherd considered Visarasarumar's command to be the command of the village community and abandoned the profession of cow grazing. Visarasarumar said, "I will now graze these cows myself," didn't he? As soon as the cowherd left, he himself undertook the task of cow grazing. Grazing cows is the traditional profession of cowherds. Grazing cows is not one of the professions prescribed for Brahmins. Brahmins are not enjoined to graze cows. It is a traditional profession belonging to cowherds. Nevertheless, here, the cowherd, whose traditional profession was grazing cows, ceases to graze them. Visarasarumar, who came from the Brahmin tradition and was not engaged in the profession of cow grazing, now decides to graze cows. Why did this happen? The answer to this is found in the previous verse. It happens according to the rules we saw, that grazing cows is a superior duty to any other profession or duty, and that this duty of cow grazing is also a path to Shiva worship. Having said this, Visarasarumar obtained the approval of the other Brahmins in the village and began to graze cows. This is because the cows in that village belonged to various Brahmin families. Therefore, he needed their consent to perform this duty of protecting cows. Furthermore, cow grazing is not a traditional profession for Brahmins. Therefore, to do something that is not a traditional profession for Brahmins, he needed the approval of the Brahmins of that village. Therefore, for these two reasons, Visarasarumar obtained the approval of the other Brahmins in the village and began to perform the duty of protecting cows, saying, "I will now graze these cows myself." Furthermore, the word "Pasu" (cow) also has the meaning of "soul" here. Therefore, when Visarasarumar said, "I will now graze these cows myself," it was not limited to cows but also had the inner meaning that "I will save all of us, who are souls." This is because, while the Nayanar was engaged in this task of grazing cows, the Shiva Puja he performed, the Chandesa position he attained through that Shiva Puja, and the benefit of Shiva Puja that the world would receive by worshipping him, were all yet to happen. Therefore, when the Nayanar says, "I will graze cows," it has the inner meaning that he will save the great multitude of souls. Thus, Sekkizhar Perumaan praises Visarasarumar, also known as Chandesura Nayanar, as "Dheiva Marai Siruvar" (the divine Vedic child) who came to protect the herds of cows. We will continue to contemplate how Visarasarumar grazed cows and how he protected them. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 10 — Chandesura Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periyapuranam, Part 254. Mummaiyaal Ulaganda Sarukkam, Chandesa Nayanar Puranam. Visarasarumar, also known as Chandesa Nayanar, takes on the duty of grazing cattle himself and begins to graze them. As we have already seen, Visarasarumar was aware of the greatness of cows. He knew and understood how the greatness of cows was described in the Vedas, Agamas, and other scriptures. Therefore, he grazed the cows accordingly. The Shiva Dharmottaram, a text, describes how cows should be protected. According to it, Visarasarumar protected the cows. In the Shiva Dharmottaram, the qualities of protecting cows are specially explained under the chapter "Gopuraviyal." The Shiva Dharmottaram is a text in Tamil, compiled by Maraignanasambandha Nayanar, which is the essence of the Shivagamas. In the Gopuraviyal section of this text, Maraignanasambandha Perumaan has graciously explained the methods of protecting cows. Let us see them as follows: When protecting and grazing cows, one must protect and graze them without causing them any harm. One must protect cows from fear. One must gently stroke them with a stick made of palasa wood and urge them forward with "Go, go." One should not harm them by getting angry, scolding, or beating them without compassion. Those who harm them in such a way will go to hell. Those who understand the greatness of cows, worship them, and offer them grass will attain Shivaloka. We can see in Thirumooladeva Nayanar's Thirumandiram that one should offer a mouthful of grass to a cow, which is beneficial for all. Thirumoolar, who was a Shivayogi, did this very act to alleviate the suffering of cows. The Shivayogi left his body and entered the body of Moolan, a cowherd, and grazed the cows. Thus, the Shivayogi entered the body of Moolan, the cowherd, through Parakaya Pravesam (entering another's body), protected the cows, and made them happy. We will see all these stories in the Puranam of Thirumooladeva Nayanar. Furthermore, the Shiva Dharmottaram states that even touching cows will remove all our evil deeds and increase all good deeds. Cows bestow both worldly and otherworldly benefits upon people. By providing the means for Shiva worship, cows also help us attain Shiva's feet. To protect cows, cow sheds (goshalas) must be built according to rules. The floor of the goshala should be made of pure soil, such as river sand, ant-hill soil, stream soil, soil from the base of a peepal tree, or soil from the base of a bilva tree. In the goshala, separate places should be arranged for young calves, mature calves, and sick calves. We have contemplated this briefly in the Puranam of Anaya Nayanar. Every day, the cow urine and cow dung in the goshala should be removed and cleaned. To prevent mosquitoes, incense should be offered, lamps should be lit, and the goshala should be decorated with flowers. Cows should be gently led into it with the word "Swasthi" and offered grass with love. Sick cows should be kept in a separate place, given medicine, and protected. It is a great meritorious act to have compassion for weak cows and buy and raise them. On every Ashtami day, cows should be bathed, adorned with flowers, offered rice and water, shown incense and lamps, worshipped, and revered. During summer, cows should be grazed in good groves. During the rainy season, they should be grazed in forest areas with light rain, and during winter, in sunny places, and protected from suffering. They should be grazed in lands with good grass and allowed to drink water from pure and good water sources. Those who do not protect cows from suffering, those who do not worship cows, and kings who do not punish those who do not protect cows will go to hell. Stones and pillars should be erected for cows to rub against. That is, stones should be erected at various places on the roads where cows walk. Thus, grazing cows will rub their bodies against these stones and find comfort. Such stones and pillars should be erected at various places. Cows should be donated to Lord Shiva and to acharyas. A bull with good characteristics should be donated to the Shiva shrine. Donating bulls for Lord Shiva's service is also a great Shiva Punya. Those who load burdens on barren cows, those who load burdens on bulls, and those who cause suffering to bulls by riding on them will fall into hell and suffer. Shivakavimani Ayya writes that Sekkizhar Nayanar has indicated in many places the tradition that bulls should not be used even for plowing. Therefore, it is implicitly understood that burdens should not be loaded on cows. Offering the five sacred ablutions (Panchagavya) given by cows to Lord Shiva is a supreme Shiva Punya. When a cow calves, the calf should be allowed to drink its mother's milk for two months, and only then should the milk be offered to Lord Shiva for sacred ablution. When the calf has drunk milk and left the udder, the udder should be washed with water before milking. Those who milk the mother cow out of greed, without leaving milk for the calf, will fall into hell and suffer for a long time, then be born on earth, suffer from severe hunger, and beg from house to house. Even if they beg, they will suffer without getting it. The milk of a Kapil (brownish-yellow) cow should be given only to Lord Shiva. Those who drink that milk without doing so will fall into hell. Untouchables should not enter cow sheds. If they do, they will fall into the hell of burning mouths for countless ages and suffer without release. If enemies seize cows, they should be resisted and protected. If it is not possible to protect them, those who sacrifice their lives in the attempt to protect cows will attain Shivapadam. Sekkizhar Perumaan, in the Periyapuranam, instructs us on the methods of protecting cows and their greatness through the stories of three Nayanars: Anaya Nayanar, Chandesura Nayanar, and Thirumooladeva Nayanar. The Vedas and Agamas speak of this special quality. These are extensively discussed in the Shiva Dharmottaram. The worship of cows is also prescribed for Saivites in the Nitya Karma Vidhi Paddhatis. These are all the methods of protecting cows mentioned in the Shiva Dharmottaram. Shrimath Arumuga Navalar has also mentioned these in his commentary on the Puranam of Chandesura Nayanar. Furthermore, Shivakavimani Ayya writes the following commentary: The Vedas, Agamas, and many other texts speak uniformly about the greatness of cow protection and the evil of not protecting cows. Those who live according to the teachings of these texts are from our sacred land of Bharata Khanda. However, in this era, all these disciplines have changed. Today, it is said that it is a great meritorious act to simply not slaughter cows for food, let alone protect them. Is this not the cruelty of time? Scriptures state that even if a single drop of cow's blood falls on the ground, millions of asuras will arise from it and destroy the world. But every day, the blood of cows slaughtered to fill human stomachs flows like a river.

No. What a chaotic state this country is in now! The wise believe that the day this terrible sin began in our sacred land was the day countless sufferings began for our country. There are people of other religions who are merciless towards cows, who slaughter cows, and who eat cow meat. It is a great sin to sell cows or calves to them, or to killers, untouchables, and so on. The one who sells cows to someone who commits such a sin is also responsible for the sin committed by the buyer. This is a truth stated in the scriptures of wisdom. Even selling sick cows, old cows, weak cows, and considering the small profit from them as wealth, is a sin. Those who own such sick cows, old cows, weak cows, and cows that have stopped giving milk, should themselves keep and protect those cows. That is their duty. If they cannot protect them, it is proper to entrust them to those who can protect cows. All those who cannot protect cows individually should gather together and establish cow protection sheds and try to protect them through that, which is also a great duty and a Shiva Punya. It is also meritorious to create lands with grass for cows to graze in outside the village. Conversely, seizing lands with grass where cows graze is a great sin. If the people of today understand and try to reform these things, it will be beneficial for the country and for us. Thus, Shivakavimani Ayya writes his commentary. We will continue to contemplate how Sekkizhar Perumaan sings about how Chandesura Nayanar grazed cows. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 11 — Chandesura Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periya Puranam, Part 255. The section on "Mummaiyaal Ulaganda Sarukkam" (The Chapter of Ruling the World with Threefold Virtues). The Puranam of Chandesura Nayanar. Sekkizhar Peruman describes the form that Chandesura Nayanar took to graze the cows. Visarasarmanar, also known as Chandesura Nayanar, appears with a staff and a rope in his hand for grazing the cows. It is essential for those who graze cows to have a rope and a staff in their hands. A rope is needed to tie cows and calves when necessary. A staff or stick helps to guide the cows. Previously, the cowherd who grazed the cows also carried a staff in his hand. Now, Visarasarmanar also takes on the form of a cowherd with a staff in his hand to graze the cows. The staff used for grazing cows should only be used to guide them, not to harm them as the earlier cowherd did. We can recall here that the very staff held by Visarasarmanar later became an axe, with which he severed his father's feet. Visarasarmanar, who set out to graze the cows, goes with the appearance that signifies his brahmacharya (celibate student) state: a topknot with a tuft of hair, a deer skin, a triple sacred thread, and a loincloth. Brahmacharis should have a topknot with a tuft of hair. This small tuft of hair is a distinguishing mark of brahmacharis. It is called Panchasikai. Next, Sekkizhar sings, "deer skin and triple sacred thread adorning his small chest." Tying a deer skin to the sacred thread and wearing it is a custom for brahmacharis. He wears a loincloth around his waist. Wearing a loincloth around the waist is also a brahmachari custom. That loincloth should be pure and white. Visarasarmanar wears such a loincloth. Thus, Visarasarmanar, with the staff and rope for grazing cows in his hand, with his small tuft of hair swaying, with the deer skin and sacred thread adorning his small chest, and with a shining loincloth around his waist, grazes the cows so that milk and calves may increase. The benefits derived from cows are milk and calves. Of these, milk is an immediate benefit, available daily. A calf is considered a long-term, great benefit. These are the two benefits of grazing cows. Visarasarmanar grazes the cows so that both of these may increase. What does it mean for milk and calves to increase? It means that both should increase equally, without one diminishing the other. In these times, many, out of greed for milk, milk the cows without leaving any for the calves, thus ruining the calves' growth. We also see the cruel sight today of people making a fake calf out of straw and showing it to the cow to milk it. Others might only want calves and waste the milk. However, here it is implied that one should graze cows in such a way that both milk and calves increase, not like these examples. Thus, he grazes the cows so that both milk and calves increase. He gives the cows as much grass as they need, allowing them to eat. During times of abundant grass, he lets the cows graze in those areas and eat the grass.
